- [decision trees: run each branch, ratio, retry, keep all] `2026-09-28T23:06:49.251Z` (session 333a74f6, line 2915)
  > this maps better to how my brain actually works,,, if in a run i have to make a guess,,, i can run down that tree and see where it likely ends and save that... then I can backtrack to the decision point and make a different choice there, I can run that tree down till it ends and save it... for me,,,, (and i think every human is different based on their learned processes) I can take something I know very little about and run 12 to 15 major decisions deep, and each of the decsisions might have 6-8 varibles, i can run all of those and deduce a ratio of what is the most correct "likely" path to the correct answer.... important,,, those decision gates might not be sequential.. there might be know processes in between... i.e. for problem A the first decision might be b,c,d,e.... if I answer b then f,g,h,i,j,k are answered, but i has a decision between j,k,l,m ... let's say i test j first, and the next level is a list of varibles of which I know they all won't work for the scope of the bigger problem, k has two varibles, both might work, l, two levels deeper is clear it won't work, m next level deep might work, but is unclear.. I might deduce that route b might solve it as long as we choose k or m... but then I go back and do the same each a,c,d and the outcome might be a ratio... a looks about 90% , b 8%, c,d are both less than 1% likely so then we test A and it actually doesn't work but it spawned a new route at the top that might be possible. N.. then I rerun the tree from the top... now a is out, (not gone, but out for now) b,c,d,n are tested, and this time,, the ratio comes back b 70%, n= 25%, c and d both grew to 2.5% ... hmm so we try b and it doesn't work but now spawned A again with a new varible deep in the tree that we didn't know existed, b is out right now, c , d and n are all tested, and A under the new choice deep in the tree becomes 99.9 % correct ... so we try A and it works,, it's adobted to be the know answer for the question, but all the other considerations are still in the memory incase i every need to hash them again.

- [fall-off: my brain flags stale memories; compress to long term] `2026-10-05T22:30:04.994Z` (session 89b642a9, line 7198) *(owner input line (`❯`) inside a pasted session tail)*
  > On the fall-off choice, I think that there's a mechanism in my brain that chooses, that just kind of walks around and looks for, or maybe there's a flag that shows up: Hey, listen, this memory hasn't been used in X amount of time. A. It has been superseded four times. B. And it is basically stale. It's a memory we should keep, but we don't need to keep it in active memory. We should store it in a compressed format in long-term storage so that we're not clogging up active memory. And I want to say—I would say that five changes. So five rows in a memory, in active memory. The sixth row, the further back—not the new row, but the further back row—drops off into long-term storage. And that's additive. So, we're not going to have every time something drops off, it's not going to make a new compressed format. It's going to add to the other compressed format, right? Which might mean that we have to unbox the compression, add that row, then rebox the compression. That's fine. And get rid of the old. That's fine. But this is how we combat bloat, is if memories— Now, you have to understand I'm talking about rows here. I'm not talking about actual memories. I think that this also transpires into actual memories. If we're using the word the 100,000 times a day, we don't need 100,000 timestamps in our memories. Some of those timestamps can just go away, right? Go to long-term memory, or, I mean honestly, if a word is locked in enough and it doesn't change over time, then maybe we don't keep track. I don't keep track of how many times I use the word the, or it, or a, or and, or any of those things. They're just part of my normal vocabulary, my normal speech. And yes, I know I need to know how to use them. I need to have those memories of, like, should I use the word the here, or should I use the word and there? I don't think about those because those words are so ingrained in the graph, they never move because they've been used so many times. Right? And so the purpose of this is, like, we counteract bloat by not storing everything, because we have different types of memories. We have procedural memories. We have word memories. We have math memory. We have, you know, different types of brains that work. I mean think about the human anatomy and the hippocampus and the different cortices and the different this and that. Like, we have to have all of those things. Those things all have jobs. Read about it. Understand it...

# Owner quotes, part 3 (messages 2026-10-11 to 2026-10-16)

Source: owner_part3.txt (435 message headers). Most of this slice is task notifications, courier relays, compaction summaries and Claude subagent reports; about 120 headers hold words the owner typed himself. Sections A to E quote only those typed words (plus a few owner lines that Claude compaction summaries quote verbatim; each is labelled "summary-quoted"). Every quote below was checked as an exact substring of its message by script.

Section Z holds second-hand quotes: owner words from his earlier owner_source / prose files, as quoted inside Claude subagent reports that arrived in this slice (2026-10-15 and 2026-10-16). They are not counted in A to E. File tags are the agent's ([112], [FD:2076], [54-dict] etc.). Text between the quote marks is the owner's; words outside the quote marks (such as "Also:" or "/") are the agent's joiners. HTML escapes in those reports were decoded.

One message (2026-10-13T09:08:11.950Z) ends with a personal self-harm remark; that last sentence is left out of the quote below and marked. On 2026-10-15T09:02:51.899Z he also wrote "fuck life sucks and then you die" (kept, in E).
